GUIDE TO APPLYING FOR REVIEW
The Administrative Appeals Tribunal (AAT) can review a wide range of decisions made under
Commonwealth laws by the Australian Government and some non-government bodies. We also
review decisions made under Norfolk Island laws.
You should use this guide if you want to apply for a review of a decision in the AAT’s General
Division, Freedom of Information Division, National Disability Insurance Scheme Division, Security
Division, Small Business Taxation Division, Taxation & Commercial Division or Veterans’ Appeals
Division.

The five parts of this guide will:
     •    help you choose which application form to use (Part 1)
     •    help you complete the application form (Part 2)
     •    help you work out if you must pay an application fee and, if so, how much (Part 3)
     •    give you some other information about applying to the AAT (Part 4) and
     •    show you where to send your form and how to contact the AAT (Part 5).
If you want to apply for a review of a decision in the AAT’s Migration & Refugee Division or Social
Services & Child Support Division, go to www.aat.gov.au and follow the links on the website.

PART 1: CHOOSING WHICH APPLICATION FORM TO USE
The AAT has three application forms to apply for a review of a decision in the AAT’s General Division,
Freedom of Information Division, National Disability Insurance Scheme Division, Security Division,
Small Business Taxation Division, Taxation & Commercial Division or Veterans’ Appeals Division.

Application for Review of Decision (Individual)
Use this form if you are an individual, unless you want to apply for a second review of a decision of the
AAT’s Social Services & Child Support Division.

Application for Review of Decision (Organisation)
Use that form if you are an organisation.

Application for Second Review of Decision
Use that form if you want to apply for a second review of a decision made by the AAT’s Social
Services & Child Support Division:
     •    about a Centrelink decision (except an employer-related paid parental leave decision)
     •    to refuse an extension of time to apply for a child support review
     •    about a person’s percentage of care for a child in a child support review.

For information about completing this form, see Part 2 of this guide.

PART 2: COMPLETING THE APPLICATION FORM
Read this guide and the form carefully. If you need more space to answer a question, continue your
answer on another sheet of paper and attach it to the form.

If you need help filling out the form or if you need more information, ask us. When you have completed
the form, you can bring it to the AAT in person or send it to us by post, email or fax. Our contact
details are at the end of this guide.




                                                         4
Administrative Appeals Tribunal / Page i of vi   www.aat.gov.au                        Information date 1 July 2022
                                                                                                                T1
APPLICANT

CONTACT DETAILS
The form asks you to give us your address, phone numbers, and any email addresses or fax numbers.
Please tell us your preferred method for receiving written information from us by ticking the box. If you
have given us an email address and you do not tick a box, we will email the documents to you.

REPRESENTATIVE
You can represent yourself at the AAT or any person you choose can represent you. If you tell us that
you have a representative, we will send the letters and other documents about your case to your
representative instead of sending them to you.

INTERPRETER
If you need an interpreter, we will arrange for a qualified interpreter to assist you free of charge.
If you speak a particular dialect, please include this in the form.

DISABILITY
If you have a disability or special need and would like some assistance, we will try to make appropriate
arrangements for you. Our offices (known as registries) have portable hearing loops and are
wheelchair accessible.

DECISION

DATE YOU RECEIVED THE DECISION
We ask you to tell us on the form when you received the decision. We need this information so we can
check if your application has been lodged within the time limit.

The time limit for lodging an application for a review of a decision is usually 28 days after you receive
the decision. For some types of decisions, the time limit is longer and for others it is shorter. The time
limit is usually stated in the information given to you by the decision-maker about your review rights.

If the time limit has expired, you can apply for an extension of time to make your application. We can
extend most, but not all, time limits. You can use the Application for an Extension of Time for Making
an Application for Review of Decision form which is on our website.

SEND US A COPY OF THE DECISION
If you can, send us a copy of the decision you want us to review. If you cannot, describe the decision
briefly, for example: ‘The Tax Office decided that I have to pay a tax debt’.

WHO MADE THE DECISION
If you are not sending us a copy of the decision, you need to tell us the name and address of the
department or organisation that made the decision.

DATE THE DECISION WAS MADE AND DECISION REFERENCE
If you are not sending us a copy of the decision, please tell us on the form when the decision was
made and to give us a decision reference number. You will usually find a reference number on a
covering letter or in the decision itself. Giving us these details helps us to identify the decision quickly.

REASONS YOU ARE MAKING AN APPLICATION
You must tell us briefly why you want to have the decision reviewed. For example, you may think the
decision is wrong and a different decision should be made, or the information you provided was not
taken into account, or the law was not applied correctly. We cannot start the review if you do not
answer this question.


                                                            5
Administrative Appeals Tribunal / Page ii of vi   www.aat.gov.au                     Information date 1 July 2022
                                                                                                              T1
TAX DECISIONS
If you are applying for review of a tax decision, we need additional information to decide how the AAT
will handle your application and the application fee you must pay.

If the tax decision relates to a small business entity, we will handle the review in our Small Business
Taxation Division. In general, you are a small business entity if you were carrying on a business and
your aggregated turnover was less than $10 million. In most cases, the letter from the Australian
Taxation Office (ATO) telling you about its decision will state whether or not they consider you are a
small business entity. For more information, please read the AAT’s Guide to the Small Business
Taxation Division which can be found on our website.

If the decision does not relate to a small business entity, we will handle your review in our Taxation &
Commercial Division.

The application fee you must pay depends on:
     •    the type of tax decision you want us to review, and
     •    the amount of tax in dispute, and
     •    whether the decision relates to a small business entity.

Please read the next section to find out what fee you must pay.

PART 3: APPLICATION FEES AND REFUNDS
You do not have to pay a fee when you apply to us for review of some types of decisions, such as
applications about Commonwealth workers’ compensation, family assistance or social security
payments, military compensation, the National Disability Insurance Scheme or veterans’ pensions.

For the review of other types of decisions, you must pay an application fee.

STANDARD APPLICATION FEE
The standard application fee is $1,011.

APPLICATION FEES FOR REVIEW OF TAX DECISIONS
A lower fee of $100 is payable for the review of the following tax decisions:
     •    the amount of tax in dispute is less than $5,000
     •    the ATO has refused your request to be released from paying a tax debt (regardless of the
          amount involved)
     •    the ATO has refused to extend the time for you to lodge an objection.

If the decision relates to a small business entity and you are not eligible to pay the lower fee of $100,
the application fee is $543.

For other applications for review of tax decisions, the standard application fee of $1,011 is payable.

CONCESSIONAL FEE
You can pay a reduced fee of $100 instead of the $1,011 fee or the $543 fee if:
     •    you have been granted legal aid for your application
     •    you hold a health care card, pensioner concession card or Commonwealth seniors health card
     •    you are in prison or lawfully detained in a public institution
     •    you are under 18 years of age or receiving youth allowance, Austudy or ABSTUDY, or
     •    we decide that paying the full fee would cause you financial hardship.


                                                             6
Administrative Appeals Tribunal / Page iii of vi   www.aat.gov.au                  Information date 1 July 2022
                                                                                                               T1
To apply for a fee reduction on the grounds of financial hardship, you must fill out the Request for Fee
Reduction form, which is on our website, and send it to us.

APPLICATIONS FOR REVIEW OF MORE THAN ONE DECISION
If the same person (an individual, organisation or other entity) applies for review of more than one
decision that relates to them and we decide that the applications can be dealt with together, we can
order that you only have to pay one fee.

If you are applying for review of more than one tax decision and different application fees would be
payable if the applications were handled separately, you must usually pay the highest of the fees.
However, if one of the fees is the $1,011 fee and the other is the $543 fee, you pay the $543 fee.

WHEN THE FEE MUST BE PAID
Application fees must be paid when the application is lodged. The AAT may dismiss your application if
you do not pay the fee within six weeks after lodging the application.

HOW TO PAY A FEE
You can pay a fee in cash or by cheque, money order, EFTPOS or credit card. We accept payment by
MasterCard and Visa.

All payment types are accepted at our registries. Credit card payments can also be made by phone.

REFUNDS
We will refund:
     •    the entire application fee if you were not required to pay it
     •    the difference between the fee you paid and a lower fee if the lower fee was payable
     •    if you pay the $1,011 fee or the $543 application fee and the application is resolved in your
          favour, the difference between the fee you paid and $100. There is no refund if you pay the
          lower application fee of $100 or the reduced fee of $100.

For more information about fees, including when there is no fee and whether you are eligible to pay a
reduced fee, go to the AAT website or contact your local AAT registry.

PART 4: OTHER INFORMATION ABOUT APPLYING TO THE AAT
APPLYING TO SUSPEND A DECISION
In general, a decision continues to operate while we are reviewing it. In some cases, we can order that
the decision be suspended while the review is taking place.

If you want to ask us to suspend the operation of the decision, you must complete a Request for Stay
Order form, which is on our website, and send it to us.

STEPS IN A REVIEW
In most cases, we use alternative dispute resolution (ADR) to help the parties – you and the
department or organisation that made the decision you want reviewed – try to reach agreement about
how the case should be resolved. Many cases are resolved at this stage. ADR processes are held in
private.

If agreement cannot be reached, we will hold a hearing and make a decision. Hearings are usually
open to the public.

Our procedures and the time needed to complete the review vary from case to case. We aim to have
cases finalised within 12 months.

                                                            7
Administrative Appeals Tribunal / Page iv of vi   www.aat.gov.au                    Information date 1 July 2022
                                                                                                                T1
PRIVACY – WHAT DO WE DO WITH INFORMATION GIVEN TO US?

We collect information from you to process your application and to carry out the review under the
Administrative Appeals Tribunal Act 1975.

We give a copy of the form to the department or organisation that made the decision you want
reviewed. We may also give a copy to any other person that is a party to the review.

If you give us other information during the review that another party does not have, we will usually give
them a copy.

Limited information about cases in the AAT’s General Division, Freedom of Information Division,
National Disability Insurance Scheme Division, Small Business Taxation Division, Taxation &
Commercial Division or Veterans’ Appeals Division is usually made available to the public on request
and can be accessed using eCase Search on our website. This information includes the names of the
parties and any representatives, the type of application, dates of conferences, hearings or other case
events, the types of key documents lodged by the parties and the outcome of an application.

More information is usually made publicly available if we hold a hearing and make a decision. If we
prepare a written statement of the reasons for our decision in your case, it will usually be made public
and published on the internet, including on the AustLII website (www.austlii.edu.au). For more
information about the decisions we publish, see our Publication of Decisions Policy on our website.

We can order that information be kept confidential if we believe there is good reason to do so. You can
apply for an order by writing to us stating what information you want kept confidential and why. In
some cases, legislation requires that information be kept confidential.

For more information see our fact sheet, Privacy and confidentiality at the AAT, and our Privacy
Policy. Our Privacy Policy includes information about how you can access and seek correction of your
personal information, make a complaint about the way we have handled your personal information and
how we will deal with such a complaint. Privacy and confidentiality at the AAT and our Privacy Policy
are on our website or are available from your local AAT registry.

MORE INFORMATION ABOUT THE AAT

For more information about the AAT and how we conduct reviews go to our website or call us. Our
staff can give you information about procedures but cannot give you legal advice.




                                                           8
Administrative Appeals Tribunal / Page v of vi   www.aat.gov.au                      Information date 1 July 2022

                                Notice of internal review decision
           Section 100 of the National Disability Insurance Scheme Act 2013 (NDIS Act)


Dear Mr Monks

I refer to the email received by the NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission (the NDIS Commission)
on 25 January 2023 requesting an internal review of the decision made on 20 December 2022 to
impose a banning order against your client, Ms Vida Reid, for a period of 5 years under section
73ZN(2)(a)(i) of the NDIS Act (the original decision).

I am the delegate of the NDIS Commissioner for the purposes of section 100 of the NDIS Act, and am
authorised to make a decision on this internal review.

The internal review decision
I have decided to vary the original decision. This means the banning order made against Mrs Reid
remains in place, however, it is changed or altered in some respect.

The variation I have decided to impose is to:

    1. change the legislative basis for the banning order from section 73ZN(2)(a)(i) of the NDIS Act
       to section 73ZN(2)(a)(iii) instead; and

    2. broaden the scope of the banning order to make it explicit that Mrs Reid is to have no
       involvement with or in the NDIS sector, in any capacity. This includes Mrs Reid being
       prohibited from:
           a) providing disability supports and services, directly or indirectly, to NDIS-funded
               participants;
           b) providing labour hire workers to support NDIS providers; or
           c) providing support, funding, guidance or assistance of any form to an NDIS provider.




                                  NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission
                                                  10
                                                                                                         T1.1
The banning order (as varied) remains subject to the exception of Mrs Reid being able to seek
disability supports or services for herself and her son, Mr Alex Reid.

I enclose the banning order as varied, and the reasons for my review decision at Attachment A to
this notice.

Consequences of review decision
The original banning order made against Mrs Reid is not disturbed and continues to have effect
from 5:30pm on 20 December 2022 (AWST).

The variation takes effect from 12.00am on 22 April 2023 until 12.00am on 21 December 2027
(AWST).

Engaging in conduct that breaches a banning order is a contravention of section 73ZN(10) of the
NDIS Act. A contravention of a banning order attracts a civil penalty of 1,000 penalty units for an
individual ($275,000).

External review rights
If Mrs Reid is dissatisfied with my review decision, she may apply to the Administrative Appeals
Tribunal (AAT) for a review of the decision. She must apply within 28 days after receiving this notice
of decision. Further information about applying to the AAT for a review can be found at
https://www.aat.gov.au/apply-for-a-review/national-disability-insurance-scheme-ndis or by
contacting the AAT on 1800 228 333.

Variation or revocation of banning order
Section 73ZO of the NDIS Act allows the NDIS Commissioner to vary or revoke a banning order at
any time, on her own initiative, or in response to an application by the person against whom the
order was made. If Mrs Reid is minded to request a variation or revocation of the banning order
against her, an application must be in accordance with the matters set out in section 73ZO of the
NDIS Act.

Contact details
Should you have any queries about this matter, please do not hesitate to contact Pascal Moussa,
Lawyer, on 02 9942 5726 or at pascal.moussa@ndiscommission.gov.au.

Yours sincerely




Sian Leathem

Complaints Commissioner

NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission

                     NATIONAL DISABILITY INSURANCE SCHEME ACT 2013 (CTH)
                                SECTION 73ZN BANNING ORDER


I, Sian Leathem, as delegate of the Commissioner of the NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission
under the National Disability Insurance Scheme Act 2013 (the NDIS Act), make this banning order
with respect to Vida Reid of 96 Amethyst Crescent, Mount Richon, WA 6112.

I make the order believing on reasonable grounds that Vida Reid is not suitable to be involved in the
provision of supports or services to people with disability.

Pursuant to section 73ZN(2)(a)(iii) of the NDIS Act, I prohibit Vida Reid from having any involvement
with or in the NDIS sector, in any capacity, for a period of five (5) years. This includes Vida Reid being
prohibited from:

    a) providing disability supports and services, directly or indirectly, to NDIS-funded
       participants;
    b) providing labour hire workers to support NDIS providers; or
    c) providing support, funding, guidance or assistance of any form to an NDIS provider.

This banning order is subject to the exception that Mrs Reid is able to seek disability supports or
services for herself and her son, Mr Alex Reid.

My reasons for making this order are set out in the enclosed statement of reasons.

This order, which varies the banning order made on 20 December 2022, takes effect from 12.00am
on 22 April 2023 until 12.00am on 21 December 2027 (AWST).

The original banning order made on 20 December 2022 remains effective from that date to the date
of this variation.

Complaints Commissioner

                                                                                        Attachment A



                                         Statement of Reasons

Internal review decision – section 100 of the National Disability Insurance Scheme Act 2013 (NDIS
                                               Act)

Preliminary matters

1.   This statement of reasons outlines why I have decided to vary the banning order made on 20
     December 2022 under section 73ZN(2)(a)(i) of the NDIS Act, which prohibited Ms Vida Reid
     from providing supports and services, directly or indirectly, to NDIS-funded participants for a
     period of 5 years, effective from 5:30pm on 20 December 2022.

2.   In this statement of reasons, I refer to the banning order made on 20 December 2022 as the
     original decision.

3.   Section 99(1), table item 15 of the NDIS Act confirms the original decision can be subject to
     internal review (as it is identified as a ‘reviewable decision’).

4.   Section 100(2) of the NDIS Act requires a request for review of a decision to be made within
     three months after receiving notice of the decision. I note Mrs Reid requested an internal
     review of the original decision within the statutory deadline.

5.   Section 100(6A) of the NDIS Act requires an internal reviewer to make a decision either:

        a.   confirming the reviewable decision;

        b.   varying the reviewable decision; or

        c.   setting aside the reviewable decision and substituting a new decision.

Role of the internal reviewer

6.   In reviewing the original decision, my task was to make the correct or preferable decision in
     accordance with the law and any relevant policies of the NDIS Commission. In doing so, I
     considered the evidence that was before the original decision-maker, as well as any new
     evidence that was before me at the time of making my decision.

7.   In deciding what is the correct or preferable decision, I needed to be satisfied on the balance of
     probabilities of any findings of fact that I made.

Decision

8.   I have decided to vary the original decision.

9.   The original decision is varied such that:

        a.   the legislative basis of the banning order is changed from section 73ZN(2)(a)(i) of the
             NDIS Act to section 73ZN(2)(a)(iii) to reflect my view that Mrs Reid should be banned on
             the basis she is not suitable to be involved in the provision of supports or services to


                                   NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission
                                                   13
                                                                                                           T1.1
            people with disability (as opposed to any previous, current or likely contravention(s) of
            the NDIS Act she may be linked to); and

       b.   the scope of the banning order made against Mrs Reid on 20 December 2022 is
            broadened to prohibit Mrs Reid from having any involvement with or in the NDIS
            sector, in any capacity. This includes Mrs Reid being prohibited from:

                i.    providing disability supports and services, directly or indirectly, to NDIS-funded
                      participants;

               ii.    providing labour hire workers to support NDIS providers; or

               iii.   providing support, funding, guidance or assistance of any form to an NDIS
                      provider.

10. However, the banning order (as varied) remains subject to the exception that Mrs Reid is able
    to seek disability supports or services for herself and her son, Mr Alex Reid.

11. The banning order made on 20 December 2022 (and varied on 21 April 2023), therefore, will
    remain in place until 12.00am on 21 December 2027 (AWST), unless it is varied or revoked
    earlier.

Materials on which my findings are based

12. In coming to my decision, I had regard to the following material:

       a.   the notice and statement of reasons for the original decision, dated 20 December 2022;

       b.   the NDIS Act;

       c.   the NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission Compliance and Enforcement Policy,
            version 2.0 dated June 2019;

       d.   the NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission Banning Order Policy, version 1.0 dated
            April 2022 (the NDIS Commission Banning Order Policy);

       e.   the submissions of Mr Tom Monks (Legal Practitioner Director from Tom & Co Legal Pty
            Ltd), made on behalf of Mrs Reid, dated 30 November 2022;

       f.   materials which were before the original decision-maker, particularly:

                i.    Briefing note (EC22-002000) relating to the notice of intention to make a
                      banning order against Mrs Reid;

               ii.    Briefing note (EC22-002581) relating to the decision to make a banning order
                      against Mrs Reid;

               iii.   the Magistrates Court of Western Australia Prosecution Notice, signed on 18
                      October 2022, in relation to the charges laid upon Mrs Reid;

               iv.    the Statement of Material Facts, dated 19 October 2022, prepared by the NDIA
                      and lodged in the criminal proceedings against Mrs Reid;



                                   NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission
                                                   14
                                                                                                                    T1.1
                v.      Australian Securities and Investments Commission (ASIC) Company Extracts for
                        Hybrid Hive Pty Ltd and Forward Focused Group Pty Ltd;

       g.    correspondence between the original decision-maker and Mr Monks, specifically:

                 i.     the letter from Mr Monks to the original decision-maker, dated 31 October
                        2022, seeking an extension of time to make submissions and further
                        information from the NDIS Commission;

                ii.     the letter from original decision-maker to Mr Monks, dated 15 November 2022,
                        granting an extension of time to make submissions and refusing the request for
                        further information; and

       h.    new evidence that arose during the course of the internal review, namely the sworn
             statement of Ms Cassandra Burke, dated 16 February 2023.

Relevant legislation
13. Section 73ZN(2) of the NDIS Act gives the Commissioner the discretion to make a banning order
    prohibiting or restricting a person employed or otherwise engaged by an NDIS provider as
    follows:

        ‘The Commissioner may, by written notice, make an order (a banning order) prohibiting or restricting
        a person who is or was employed or otherwise engaged by an NDIS provider, or a person who is or
        was a member of the key personnel of an NDIS provider, from engaging in specified activities if:

            (a) the Commissioner reasonably believes that:

                 i. the person has contravened, is contravening, or is likely to contravene this Act; or

                 ii.   the person has been involved in, or is likely to become involved in, a contravention of this
                       Act by another person; or

                iii. the person is not suitable to be involved in the provision of supports or services to people
                     with disability, having regard to any matters prescribed by the National Disability
                     Insurance Scheme rules for the purposes of this subparagraph; or

                iv.    there is an immediate danger to the health, safety or wellbeing of a person with
                       disability if the person continues to engage in the specified activities; or

            (b) the person is convicted of an offence involving fraud or dishonesty; or

            (c) the person becomes an insolvent under administration.’

14. Under section 73ZN(3) of the NDIS Act, a banning order may apply generally or be of limited
    application, and may be permanent or for a specified period.

15. Section 73ZN(7) of the NDIS Act states that the Commissioner may only make a banning order
    against a person after giving the person an opportunity to make submissions to the
    Commissioner on the matter (except in certain circumstances outlined in section 73ZN(8) which
    are not relevant to this matter).

16. Section 73ZN(9) sets out the various requirements surrounding any banning order made as
    follows:


                                       NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission
                                                       15
                                                                                                                T1.1
        'The Commissioner must:

            (a) give the banning order to the person against whom the order is made; and

            (b) include in the banning order a statement of reasons for the order; and

            (c) if the banning order is against a person who is employed or otherwise engaged by an NDIS
                 provider or is a member of the key personnel of an NDIS provider—notify the NDIS provider of
                 the order as soon as possible.’

Background and procedural history of matter
17. From 28 August 2020 to 1 November 2022, Mrs Reid was, at various periods, the sole director
    or co-director of Forward Focused Group Pty Ltd (FFG), which was a registered NDIS provider.

18. On 19 October 2022, the original decision-maker issued Mrs Reid with a notice of intention to
    make a banning order against her. In the letter, the original decision-maker invited Mrs Reid to
    make submissions in relation to the proposed banning order by no later than 5pm (AWST) on
    16 November 2022.

19. On the same date, the original decision-maker also decided to:

       a.     suspend the registration of FFG for a period of 30 days until 19 November 2022; and

       b.     issue FFG with a notice of intention to revoke the registration of, and make a banning
              order against the NDIS provider.

    The evidence supporting the proposed banning of Mrs Reid and the regulatory actions against
    FFG was similar or overlapped considerably.

20. On 31 October 2022, Mr Monks wrote to the original decision-maker to:

       a.     confirm he represented Mrs Reid (and FFG);

       b.     seek an extension of 28 days for Mrs Reid (and FFG) to provide submissions; and

       c.     seek a copy of the information held by the NDIS Commission in determining to suspend
              the registration of FFG. Given the evidential similarities in the matters noted above, it
              appears the original decision-maker interpreted this request to consequentially be
              seeking the material that also supported the banning order proposed to be made
              against Mrs Reid.

    Mr Monks requested the original decision-maker provide a response in relation to these
    requests within 7 days.

21. The original decision-maker did not provide a response within the requested timeframe.

22. On 15 November 2022, the original decision-maker wrote to Mr Monks granting an extension
    of time for Mrs Reid (and FFG) to make further submissions. The new deadline for providing
    submissions was by 5pm (AWST) on 30 November 2022. The original decision-maker declined
    to provide the further information sought by Mr Monks on the basis that the original decision-
    maker was satisfied the evidence supporting the various regulatory actions against FFG and Mrs
    Reid had been adequately disclosed in the notices dated 19 October 2022.



                                     NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission
                                                     16
                                                                                                        T1.1
23. On 30 November 2022, Mr Monks provided a single, joint submission for Mrs Reid and FFG.
    Those submissions allege the original decision-maker did not respond to Mr Monk’s requests
    for an extension of time and further information. This is not correct. I have been provided with
    a copy of an email, dated 15 November 2022, sent at 6:04pm, from
    WACompliance@ndiscommission.gov.au to info@tomandcolegal.com.au, which attached the
    letter described above at paragraph [22], granting an extension of time and declining the
    request for further information.

24. On 20 December 2022, the original decision-maker made a banning order against Mrs Reid,
    prohibiting her from providing supports and services, directly or indirectly, to NDIS-funded
    participants for a period of 5 years, under section 73ZN(2)(a)(i) of the NDIS Act, on the basis
    that he had reasonable grounds to believe that Mrs Reid had contravened, was contravening or
    was likely to contravene the NDIS Act. The banning order took effect from 5:30pm (AWST) on
    20 December 2022.

25. On 25 January 2023, Mr Monks, on behalf of Mrs Reid, requested an internal review of the
    decision to make a banning order on 20 December 2022.

26. On 6 February 2023, the NDIS Commission emailed Mr Monks a letter of acknowledgement in
    relation to the request for an internal review. The letter invited Mrs Reid to make submissions
    to be considered as part of the internal review by 5pm on 20 February 2023. No submissions
    were received from, or on behalf of, Mrs Reid.

27. On 22 March 2023, I wrote to Mr Monks to inform him that, in the course of conducting the
    internal review, I had become aware of information not considered by the original delegate. I
    provided Mr Monks with a copy of the new information and informed him of my intention to
    vary the banning order against Mrs Reid in light of such material. The letter invited Mrs Reid to
    make submissions to be considered as part of the internal review by 5pm on 5 April 2023. No
    submissions were received from, or on behalf of, Mrs Reid.

Findings and Reasons

28. I have considered the original decision made on 20 December 2022.

29. I am of the view that the original decision was the correct and preferable decision to be made,
    however, I disagree with the basis for the original decision, namely the original decision-maker
    forming a reasonable belief that Mrs Reid had contravened, was contravening, or likely to
    contravene the NDIS Act. Section 73ZN(2)(a)(i) allows for a banning order to be made against a
    person in such circumstances.

30. In my view, the original decision should have been made pursuant to section 73ZN(2)(a)(iii) of
    the NDIS Act, which provides a banning order can be made where a decision-maker forms a
    reasonable belief that a person is not suitable to be involved in the provision of supports or
    services to people with disability.

31. A banning order on this latter basis accords with the usual approach followed by the NDIS
    Commission in matters where a person is alleged to have engaged in fraudulent activity against
    the NDIS (particularly where criminal charges are laid) and is also more consistent with the
    NDIS Commission’s Banning Order Policy.



                                  NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission
                                                  17
                                                                                                       T1.1
32. In the paragraphs below, I set out my reasons for reaching this decision.

Consideration of material before original decision-maker

33. I acknowledge the original decision-maker had a wide range of material before him.

34. In conducting this internal review, I relied upon a circumscribed amount of that material as
    noted above at paragraph 12.

35. As a result, I made the following findings:

        a.   FFG and Hybrid Hive Pty Ltd (Hybrid) are NDIS providers with whom Mrs Reid is (or
             was) associated.

        b.   FFG was formerly a registered NDIS provider operating in Western Australia, until 20
             December 2022 when a delegate of the NDIS Commissioner revoked the registration of
             FFG, and made a banning order prohibiting FFG from providing supports or services to
             NDIS-funded participants for a period of 10 years.

        c.   In relation to FFG, Mrs Reid was:

                i.    the co-director from 28 August 2020 to 3 January 2021;

                ii.   the sole director from 4 January 2021 to 20 October 2022; and

               iii.   the co-director from 21 October 2022 to 1 November 2022.

        d.   Hybrid was formerly an unregistered NDIS provider operating in Western Australia,
             until a delegate of the NDIS Commissioner made a banning order on 21 December 2022
             permanently prohibiting Hybrid from providing supports and services to NDIS-funded
             participants.

        e.   In relation to Hybrid, Mrs Reid was:

                i.    the sole director from 1 December 2021 to 16 February 2022; and

                ii.   the co-director from 17 February 2022 to 21 August 2022.

        f.   On 19 October 2022, Mrs Reid was arrested and charged with several offences under
             sections 135.1(3) and 400.9(1) of the Criminal Code (Cth), which is contained in the
             Schedule to the Criminal Code Act 1995 (Cth). Those charges related to Mrs Reid
             allegedly defrauding the National Disability Insurance Scheme (NDIS).

        g.   Specifically, the charges were that:

                i.    between 1 December 2021 and 17 May 2022 at Perth in the State of Western
                      Australia, Mrs Reid dealt with money or other property, it being reasonable to
                      suspect that such money or other property was proceeds of crime and at the
                      time of dealing the value of the money or property was more than $100,000,
                      contrary to section 400.9(1) of the Criminal Code (Cth);

                ii.   between 26 December 2021 and 23 June 2022 at Perth in the State of Western
                      Australia, Mrs Reid dealt with money or other property, it being reasonable to


                                  NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission
                                                    18
                                                                                              T1.1
              suspect that such money or other property was proceeds of crime and at the
              time of dealing the value of the money or property was more than $100,000,
              contrary to section 400.9(1) of the Criminal Code (Cth);

       iii.   between 30 November 2021 and 8 April 2022 at Perth in the State of Western
              Australia, Mrs Reid caused payment requests to be submitted, which she knew
              to be false, to the NDIA, a Commonwealth entity, with the intention of
              dishonestly causing a loss, contrary to section 135.1(3) of the Criminal Code
              (Cth); and

       iv.    between 18 November 2021 and 19 April 2022 at Perth in the State of Western
              Australia, Mrs Reid caused payment requests to be submitted, which she knew
              to be false, to the NDIA, a Commonwealth entity, with the intention of
              dishonestly causing a loss, contrary to section 135.1(3) of the Criminal Code
              (Cth).

h.   In the criminal proceedings relating to these charges, the NDIA lodged a Statement of
     Material Facts, dated 19 October 2022, which outlined the relevant facts underlying the
     charges (the SoFM). The SoFM provided that:

        i.    Mrs Reid (along with Mr Simon Reid, her husband and business partner) are
              joint signatories to company bank accounts for FFG and Hybrid, namely:

                        ANZ Bank account numbered 016253 154173449 (the Hybrid ANZ
                         account);

                        ANZ Bank account numbered 016253 152003884 (the FFG ANZ
                         account); and

                        Westpac Bank account numbered 036032 736973 (the FFG Westpac
                         account).

        ii.   between 3 February 2021 and 18 April 2022, 40 separate complaints were
              made to the NDIA in relation to the conduct and claiming patterns of FFG and
              Hybrid.

       iii.   between 20 May 2022 and 1 September 2022, 29 separate complaints were
              made to the NDIA in relation to the conduct and claiming patterns of FFG and
              Hybrid.

       iv.    on 1 September 2022, the NDIA commenced an investigation into the claims
              submitted by FFG and Hybrid. The investigation discovered the matters set out
              below.

        v.    on 30 November 2021, the NDIA issued a new plan for NDIS participant, Ms
              Meghan Goodwin. On the same day, Ms Reid contacted the NDIA and
              requested a copy of the new plan for Ms Goodwin, which was emailed to her.
              Within an hour of making that call, FFG made three separate claims for
              payment in relation to supports and services purportedly provide to Ms
              Goodwin. The claims were submitted against the plan expiring on 30 November



                          NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission
                                          19
                                                                                        T1.1
        2021 and, in my view, appeared to be excessive and falsified. For example, the
        claims submitted included 398 hours of house cleaning between 15 October
        2021 and 30 November 2021 – this equates to 8.46 hours of cleaning every day
        for 47 days. On 1 December 2021, the NDIA made a payment of $43,004.60 into
        the FFG ANZ account, for which Mrs Reid is a joint signatory.

 vi.    Zettai Pty Ltd (trading as Assist Plan Managers) (Assist), a registered NDIS
        provider that performs the services of a Plan Manager, at various times
        received invoices from FFG and Hybrid for supports or services purportedly
        provided to various NDIS participants. Assist would then make claims to the
        NDIA for those supports or services, receive funds from the NDIA, and then
        forward those funds to FFG or Hybrid.

vii.    between 30 December 2021 and 20 April 2022, Assist submitted 14 separate
        claims to the NDIA for supports or services purportedly provided by FFG to Ms
        Goodwin. The claims resulted in the payment of $5979.64 to Assist, who
        subsequently transferred those funds to the FFG ANZ Account, for which Mrs
        Reid is a joint signatory.

viii.   between 25 January 2022 and 10 May 2022, Assist submitted 20 separate
        claims to the NDIA for supports or services purportedly provided by Hybrid to
        an NDIS participant, Ms Meghan Goodwin. The claims resulted in the payment
        of $78,491.06 to Assist, who subsequently transferred those funds to the
        Hybrid ANZ account, for which Mrs Reid is a joint signatory.

 ix.    Ms Goodwin has provided a witness statement that she did not receive the
        supports or services purportedly provided in the claims referred to above.

  x.    an NDIA forensic accountant examined (among other things) the statement of
        Ms Goodwin, NDIS payments records and banks statements for the FFG ANZ
        account and the Hybrid ANZ account. The NDIA forensic accountant calculated
        that the value of claims submitted by FFG and Hybrid where no support or
        service was provided to Ms Goodwin to be $127,475.30.

 xi.    as a result, the NDIA alleges that Mrs Reid received money reasonably
        suspected of being the proceeds of crime to the sum of $127,475.30.

xii.    between 24 November 2021 and 22 April 2022, Assist submitted 14 separate
        claims to the NDIA for supports or services purportedly provided by FFG to
        another NDIS participant, Ms Sarah-Jane Clulow. The claims resulted in the
        payment of $86,192.60 to Assist, who subsequently transferred those funds to
        the FFG ANZ Account, for which Mrs Reid is a joint signatory.

xiii.   between 24 November 2021 and 22 April 2022, Assist submitted 14 separate
        claims to the NDIA for supports or services purportedly provided by FFG to
        another NDIS participant, Ms Sarah-Jane Clulow. The claims resulted in the
        payment of $86,192.60 to Assist, who subsequently transferred those funds to
        the FFG ANZ Account, for which Mrs Reid is a joint signatory.




                     NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission
                                     20
                                                                                                        T1.1
              xiv.    between 25 May 2022 and 22 June 2022, Assist submitted 14 separate claims
                      to the NDIA for supports or services purportedly provided by FFG to Ms Clulow.
                      The claims resulted in the payment of $33,816.88 to Assist, who subsequently
                      transferred those funds to the FFG Westpac account, for which Mrs Reid is a
                      joint signatory.

               xv.    Ms Clulow has provided a witness statement that she did not receive the
                      supports or services purportedly provided in the claims referred to above.

              xvi.    an NDIA forensic accountant examined (among other things) the statement of
                      Ms Clulow, NDIS payments records and banks statements for the FFG ANZ
                      account and the FFG Westpac account. The NDIA forensic accountant
                      calculated that the value of claims submitted by FFG where no support or
                      service was provided to Ms Clulow to be $120,009.48.

              xvii.   as a result, the NDIA alleges that Mrs Reid received money reasonably
                      suspected of being the proceeds of crime to the sum of $120,009.48.

             xviii.   between 26 August 2022 and 21 September 2022, Assist submitted to the NDIA
                      11 separate reports of suspicious invoices it had received from FFG and Hybrid.


Section 73ZN(2)(a)(iii) of the NDIS Act – the suitability of Mrs Reid to provide supports and services to
people with disability

36. In light of the above information, I am of the reasonable belief that Mrs Reid is not suitable to
    be involved in the provision of supports or services to people with disability.

37. In forming this view, I have taken into consideration and balanced the following factors:

        a.   The allegations against Mrs Reid, though unproven, are extremely serious. Some of the
             offences which Mrs Reid has been charged with carry a maximum penalty of 10 years
             imprisonment. The alleged conduct falls well below the standard and quality of care
             expected from a member of the key personnel of an NDIS provider, particularly a
             director.

        b.   I acknowledge Mrs Reid has considerable experience in, and links to the disability care
             and support sector. Whilst these matters are relevant to an assessment of Mrs Reid’s
             suitability to be involved in the sector, I find that they do not outweigh the other
             factors that favour the making of a banning order, particularly the fact that Mrs Reid
             engaged in the alleged criminal conduct whilst she was the director of two NDIS
             providers. In fact, the position she held within those NDIS providers significantly
             facilitated the commission of the alleged fraud.

        c.   Section 3 of the NDIS Act contains the objects of the NDIS Act, which includes:

                 i.   supporting the independence and social and economic participation of people
                      with a disability;




                                   NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission
                                                   21
                                                                                                           T1.1
               ii.    enabling people with a disability to exercise choice and control in the pursuit of
                      their goals and the planning and delivery of their supports;

               iii.   promoting the provision of high quality and innovative supports that enable
                      people with a disability to maximise independent lifestyles and full inclusion in
                      the community; and

               iv.    protecting and preventing people with disability from experiencing harm arising
                      from poor quality or unsafe supports or services provided under the NDIS.

            In giving effect to the objects of the NDIS Act, section 3(3)(b) of the NDIS Act requires
            regard to be had to the need to ensure the financial sustainability of the NDIS.

            Similarly, as set out in section 4(17)(b) of the NDIS Act, it is the intention of Parliament
            that the Commissioner, amongst others, has regard to the need to ensure the financial
            sustainability of the NDIS, in performing her functions and exercising her powers under
            the NDIS Act.

            The allegations made against Mrs Reid by the NDIA are likely to cause harm to, or
            jeopardise, public trust in the NDIS and, if made out, are likely to impact the financial
            sustainability of the NDIS.

       d.   The need to protect the interests of people with disability, who may be particularly
            vulnerable, and ensure that they are kept safe, requires that in matters of this kind, a
            person facing such serious criminal charges must be prevented from having access to
            the NDIS plans of people of disability and delivering supports or services to them.
            Allowing Mrs Reid to continue working in the NDIS would undermine the objects of the
            NDIS, which includes protecting people with disability from experiencing harm.

       e.   I acknowledge the making of a banning order in respect of Mrs Reid will impact her
            ability to earn an income in the disability services sector, and I have taken this into
            account. However, I consider that the competing interest of ensuring NDIS participants,
            who may not be able to fully protect their own interests and who have a statutory right
            to safe services and protection from violence, abuse and exploitation, is paramount and
            takes precedence over the detriment Mrs Reid may experience if banned.

The submissions of Mrs Reid

38. I have considered the submissions made by Mr Monks, on 30 November 2022, on behalf of Mrs
    Reid.

39. As already noted, Mr Monk made a single, joint submission that addressed the allegations
    against, and regulatory action proposed to be taken in relation to, both FFG and Mrs Reid.

40. For the most part, the submissions were framed with a focus on FFG (as opposed to Mrs Reid),
    however, I have interpreted them as having been intended to apply or relate to Mrs Reid too
    given the degree of similarity between the allegations against FFG and Mrs Reid.

41. In summary, the submissions made by Mr Monks deny all allegations that Mrs Reid had
    contravened, was contravening or likely to contravene the NDIS Act, and include various



                                   NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission
                                                   22
                                                                                                       T1.1
    explanations and evidence in support of those assertions. Given my approach to this internal
    review from a suitability perspective (as opposed to contraventions of the NDIS Act), it is not
    necessary or conducive to go into a detailed examination of the submissions which related to
    contraventions of the NDIS Act.

42. This internal review was determined on the basis of Mrs Reid’s suitability to engage in the NDIS
    sector. The submissions made by Mr Monks that relate to the question of Mrs Reid’s suitability
    (or the criminal charges against her) are found on pages 7 and 11 of the submissions.

43. In summary, those submissions were that:

       a.   Mrs Reid emphatically denies the allegation that false claims were submitted for
            payment to the NDIA with the intention of dishonestly causing a loss to a
            Commonwealth entity, the NDIA;

       b.   the matter is currently the subject of criminal proceedings and it is inappropriate for
            the NDIS Commission to pursue the allegations until the criminal proceedings have
            been determined;

       c.   in the interests of ‘procedural and substantive justice’, FFG’s ‘fitness to hold an NDIS
            registration’ should not be determined until the criminal proceedings are determined;

44. I gave significant weight to these submissions as I acknowledge that the allegations against Mrs
    Reid have not been proven to the criminal standard.

45. I also acknowledge and respect the presumption of innocence in favour of Mrs Reid in relation
    to the criminal charges.

46. However, the submissions of Mr Monks ultimately did not dissuade me from my view that a
    banning order should be made against Mrs Reid. In the context of criminal proceedings, the
    standard of proof that applies is that allegations must be proven beyond a reasonable doubt.
    However, the making of a banning order, which is an administrative decision, only requires that
    I be satisfied on the balance of probabilities that Mrs Reid has engaged in the conduct alleged,
    which, I am, given the strength of the evidence referred to above (which indicates the alleged
    fraudulent conduct was systemic in nature and occurred over a prolonged period of time).

47. Furthermore, for the reasons already set out above at paragraphs at 37(a) to (e), I consider
    there would be a significant and indefensible risk associated with Mrs Reid being allowed to
    continue to engage in the NDIS sector until her criminal proceedings are determined; the
    likelihood of further harm to other NDIS participants to whom Mrs Reid may provide supports
    or services warrants the NDIS Commission taking regulatory action at this time to safeguard
    NDIS participants.

Procedural fairness

48. The notice of intention to make a banning order against Mrs Reid (dated 19 October 2022) and
    the subsequent statement of reasons in support of the original decision (dated 20 December
    2022) made reference to the criminal conduct Mrs Reid is alleged to have engaged in. However,
    neither document included the level of detail I have set out in this statement of reasons in
    relation to the alleged criminal conduct.



                                 NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission
                                                 23
                                                                                                          T1.1
49. In particular, the original decision-maker did not list the precise criminal charges laid under the
    Criminal Code (Cth) nor reference the SoFM lodged by the NDIA.

50. It is worth noting that I do not consider this poses a procedural fairness issue for the following
    reasons:

        a.   the information provided by the original decision-maker to Mrs Reid was sufficient to
             enable her to understand the nature and substance of the matters the original
             decision-maker was referring to, and taking into account, in making his decision.
             Supporting my view that Mrs Reid was sufficiently informed of the criminal allegations
             are the submissions of Mr Monks (specifically pages 7 and 11) where it is clear Mr
             Monks was able to identify the allegations of fraudulent conduct that the original
             decision-maker was alluding to, and provided a response that addressed the relevant
             criminal proceedings; and

        b.   the information I have referred to in this statement of reasons (i.e. the criminal charges
             and NDIA SoFM) are matters which Mrs Reid would already be aware of, and possess
             information in relation to. For example, Mrs Reid was given a copy of the prosecution
             notice detailing the criminal charges against her as well as a copy of the NDIA SoFM in
             the course of the criminal proceedings. She was, therefore, well placed to make
             submissions in relation to those matters.

Consideration of new evidence that arose after the original decision was made

51. In the course of undertaking this internal review, I became aware of new information that
    indicated Mrs Reid may be involved in a business (other than FFG and Hybrid) that has links to
    the NDIS, namely Dream Big Employment Pty Ltd (Dream Big). Mrs Reid’s involvement in this
    entity seems to have commenced around the time the NDIS Commission began taking
    regulatory action against the NDIS providers that she was a director of, as well as against Mrs
    Reid in a personal capacity. For example, on 19 October 2022 the registration of FFG was
    suspended by the NDIS Commission and Mrs Reid was notified that the original decision-maker
    was intending to make a banning order against her personally.

52. The ‘new’ material before me was a sworn statement by a former employee of FFG, Ms
    Cassandra Burke, dated 16 February 2023. The statement relevantly notes that:

        a.   On or around 15th November 2022, whilst working for a new employer (Access
             Disability Services), Ms Burke was informed by a client (NDIS participant, Mr Jonathon
             Shapiera) that he would be moving to another support co-ordinator, Mr Blake Sadler,
             who was going to be running a company for Mrs Reid (and her husband) called Dream
             Big; and

        b.   On 9 December 2022, Ms Burke received an email from Ms Pamela Cricelli (the mother
             of NDIS participant, Mr Giuseppe Cricelli) stating that she wished to cancel her son’s
             contract with Ms Burke as she was ‘going back to Vida and Simon’; and

        c.   On 1 December 2022, Ms Burke encountered Ms Reid outside an office in Jull Street
             and Ms Reid said to her “I would like you to come and work for my new company”,
             which Mrs Burke understood to be a reference to Dream Big.



                                  NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission
                                                  24
                                                                                                       T1.1
53. On 22 March 2023, I extended to Mrs Reid an opportunity to make submissions in relation to
    these allegations. No submissions were received from, or on behalf of, Mrs Reid.

54. In the absence of any information or evidence to negate the veracity of Ms Burke’s statement, I
    attribute considerable weight to the allegations contained in the statement.

55. I find it concerning that Mrs Reid seems to have attempted to remain involved in the NDIS
    sector in circumstances where she (and the NDIS providers she was associated with) were
    subject to significant regulatory action that was specifically intended to prohibit them from
    engaging in the NDIS. In particular, the establishment of Dream Big, which I understand
    occurred in affiliation with associates of Mrs Reid, who have been appointed as the
    officeholders for the company instead of Mrs Reid, indicates to me that Mrs Reid may be
    finding strategic or tactical ways to circumvent the terms of the banning order made against
    her and that it is necessary to broaden the scope of the banning order accordingly.

The NDIS Commission Banning Order Policy

56. The NDIS Commission Banning Order Policy contemplates that a banning order can be ‘issued
    pending the determination of [criminal] charges’ if, in the circumstances, the information and
    evidence obtained by the NDIS Commission supports a view that a banning order is warranted
    and appropriate.

57. In practice, this is ordinarily operationalised by the NDIS Commission making the banning order
    under section 73ZN(2)(a)(iii) of the NDIS Act as opposed to the other grounds set out in the
    NDIS Act. The reason being that criminal wrongdoing (whether alleged or proven) directly
    relates to the suitability of the relevant person to engage in the NDIS sector and section
    73ZN(2)(a)(iii) is therefore the most relevant statutory provision in those circumstances.

58. Therefore, for the reasons noted above (particularly in paragraphs 37(a) to (e) and 51 to 55), I
    consider a banning order against Mrs Reid to be warranted and appropriate.

59. In my view, there is no compelling reason to diverge from the usual practice of the NDIS
    Commission making such an order under section 73ZN(2)(a)(iii) of the NDIS Act and aberrantly
    relying upon a different statutory basis.

Requirement to take proportionate and risk responsive action under NDIS Act

60. The NDIS Commission is required to conduct compliance and enforcement activities in a risk
    responsive and proportionate manner (section 181D(4)(b) of the NDIS Act).

61. The NDIS Commission’s Banning Order Policy notes that a banning order can have a significant
    impact on the recipient and on people with disability, and as such, it will be reserved for
    circumstances where it is needed to protect people with disability from the risk of being
    exposed to conduct that falls well short of acceptable standards. However, a banning order
    should not be for a longer period than is considered necessary.

62. Having regard to the criminal proceedings presently pending against Mrs Reid, and in particular
    the serious and systemic nature of the allegations raised in those proceedings, I consider Mrs
    Reid is not suitable, at this time, to be an NDIS provider.




                                  NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission
                                                  25
                                                                                                    T1.1
63. I consider that a banning order is appropriate in this case. The banning order will remain in
    place until 12:00am (AWST) on 21 December 2027. The period of the banning order
    corresponds with the amount of time it is reasonably expected to take for the criminal
    proceedings to be finally determined by the court. Upon the determination of those
    proceedings, the banning order can be revisited and consideration given to whether it should
    be varied or revoked entirely.

64. In the meantime, the banning order will protect NDIS participants from the risk of being
    exposed to fraudulent claims and having the funds in their NDIS plans depleted.

Conclusion

    65. The NDIS Provider Register published on the NDIS Commission’s website will be updated to
        reflect the varied banning order made against Mrs Reid.




Sian Leathem

Complaints Commissioner

Delegate of the NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commissioner

NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission



21 April 2023

                              STATEMENT PURSUANT TO SECTION 37 OF
                          THE ADMINISTRATIVE APPEALS TRIBUNAL ACT 1975

1.     An application has been lodged (T1) for review of a decision of the respondent dated
       21 April 2023 (T1.1), which varied the determination/banning order of 20 December
       2022 to change the legislative basis for the banning order to section 73ZN(2)(a)(iii) and
       broaden the scope of the banning order under s 73ZO of the National Disability
       Insurance Scheme Act 2013 (the NDIS Act).

DETERMINATION OF 20 DECEMBER 2022 (T14)

2.     On 20 December 2022, a delegate issued a Notice of Banning Order to the applicant
       under s 73ZN of the NDIS Act. The delegate reasonably believed that the applicant
       had contravened, was contravening, or was likely to contravene the NDIS Act in
       accordance with s 73ZN(2)(a)(i) of the NDIS Act.

3.     The banning order prohibited the applicant from being involved in the provision of
       supports or services to people with disability for a period of five (5) years.

4.     The banning order did not apply in relation to any supports or services that the
       applicant may seek for herself or that she may provide to her son.

5.     On 25 January 2023, the applicant requested reconsideration of the determination
       dated 20 December 2022 (T16).

REVIEWABLE DECISION OF 21 APRIL 2023 (T1.1)

6.     On 21 April 2023, a delegate of the respondent varied the determination of 20
       December 2022 to:

       6.1. change the legislative basis for the banning order from section 73ZN(2)(a)(i) of
            the NDIS Act to s 73ZN(2)(a)(iii) instead; and

       6.2. broaden the scope of the banning order to make it explicit that the applicant is to
            have no involvement with or in the NDIS sector, in any capacity. This includes
            the applicant being prohibited from:
